# Islamic State

This article is about the modern Salafi jihadist organisation. For the concept of a state based on Islamic law, see [Islamic state](2646438.md).

Several terms redirect here. For other uses, see [ISIL (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIL_\(disambiguation\)), [Isis (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isis_\(disambiguation\)), and [Daesh (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daesh_\(disambiguation\)).

The **Islamic State** (**IS**),[^cite_note-109] [also known as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_the_Islamic_State) the **Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant** (**ISIL**),[^cite_note-112] the **Islamic State of Iraq and Syria** (**ISIS**), and by its Arabic acronym **Da'esh**, is a transnational [Salafi jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism) militant organisation and internationally unrecognised former [quasi-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quasi-state).[^cite_note-\:0-113][^cite_note-guardian-traincamp-114] IS occupied a significant amount of territory in [Syria](7515849.md) during the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) and in [Iraq](7515928.md) after the [U.S. troops withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\)). In 2014 the group proclaimed itself to be a worldwide [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) and claimed religious and political authority over all [Muslims](19541.md) worldwide, a claim not accepted by the vast majority of Muslims. It is [designated as a terrorist organisation](63578.md) by the [United Nations](31769.md), making it subject to binding counterterrorism sanctions in [every UN member state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_member_state), including all [Muslim countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]&#x20;

By the end of 2015, its self-declared caliphate ruled an area with a population of about 12 million,[^cite_note-119] where they enforced their extremist interpretation of [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law), managed an annual budget exceeding US\$1 billion, and commanded more than 30,000 fighters.[^cite_note-120] After a grinding conflict with American, Iraqi, and Kurdish forces, IS lost control of its Middle Eastern territories by 2019, subsequently reverting to insurgency from remote hideouts while continuing its [propaganda efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_use_by_the_Islamic_State). These efforts have garnered a significant following in northern and [Sahelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahelian) Africa,[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] where IS still controls a significant territory.[^cite_note-C-T-K-123][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20242,_6-124]&#x20;

Originating in the [Jaish al-Ta'ifa al-Mansurah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish_al-Ta%27ifa_al-Mansurah) founded by [Abu Omar al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Omar_al-Baghdadi) in 2004, the organisation (primarily under the [Islamic State of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq) name) affiliated itself with [al-Qaeda in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_Iraq) and fought alongside them during the [2003–2006 phase of the Iraqi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003%E2%80%932006_phase_of_the_Iraqi_insurgency). The group later changed its name to Islamic State of Iraq and Levant for about a year,[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] before declaring itself to be a worldwide [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate),[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-newname-128] called simply the Islamic State (الدَّولةُ الإِسْلَامِيَّة, *ad-Dawlah al-Islāmiyya*).[^cite_note-BBCSep2014-129]&#x20;

During its rule in Syria and Iraq, the group became notorious for its brutality.[^cite_note-BBCSep2014-129] It committed [genocides against Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidi_genocide) and [Iraqi Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmen_genocide); engaged in [persecution of Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_by_the_Islamic_State), [Shia Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Shias_by_the_Islamic_State), and [Mandaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandaeans); publicised [videos of beheadings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_beheadings) of soldiers, journalists, and aid workers; and [destroyed cultural sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_cultural_heritage_by_the_Islamic_State). The group has perpetrated terrorist massacres in territories outside of its control, such as the [November 2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks), the [2024 Kerman bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Kerman_bombings) in Iran, and the [2024 Crocus City Hall attack](76418995.md) in Russia. [Lone wolf attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lone_wolf_attack) inspired by the group have also taken place.&#x20;

After 2015, the [Iraqi Armed Forces](4627429.md) and the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) pushed back IS and degraded its financial and military infrastructure,[^cite_note-130] assisted by advisors, weapons, training, supplies, and airstrikes by the [American-led coalition](44120613.md) and [Iranian proxy forces](36658251.md),[^cite_note-once_promised-131][^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] and later by [Russian airstrikes, bombings, cruise missile attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war), and [scorched-earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorched_earth) tactics across Syria, which focused mostly on razing [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition) strongholds rather than IS bases.[^cite_note-134] By March 2019, IS lost the last of its territories in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia), although its affiliates maintain a significant territorial presence in Africa as of 2025.[^cite_note-C-T-K-123][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20242,_6-124][^cite_note-135]&#x20;

## &#x20;Name

Main article: [Names of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_the_Islamic_State)

See also: [Name changes due to the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_changes_due_to_the_Islamic_State)

The Islamic State, abbreviated IS,[^cite_note-136] is [also known as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_the_Islamic_State) the Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant (ISIL [/ˈaɪsɪl/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*EYE-sil*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key)), the Islamic State of Iraq and Syria (ISIS [/ˈaɪsɪs/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*EYE-siss*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key)),[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-ISIS_or_ISIL?_The_debate-138] and by its [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) acronym Daesh[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] (داعش, *Dāʿish*, IPA: [\[ˈdaːʕɪʃ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic)),[^cite_note-DAESH_shift-141] and also as *Dawlat al-Islām* (دولة الإسلام).[^cite_note-142] In April 2013, having expanded into Syria, the group adopted the name *ad-Dawla al-Islāmiyya fī l-ʿIrāq wa-sh-Shām* (الدَّولةُ الإِسْلَامِيَّة فِي ٱلْعِرَاق وَٱلشَّام). As *[al-Shām](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D8%A7%D9%85)* is a region often compared with the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) or the [region of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)), the group's name has been variously translated as "Islamic State of Iraq and [al-Sham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Sham)",[^cite_note-BBC_2_December_2015-143] "Islamic State of Iraq and Syria" (both abbreviated as ISIS),[^cite_note-144] or "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant" (abbreviated as ISIL).[^cite_note-ISIS_or_ISIL?_The_debate-138] In 2014, [Dar al-Ifta al-Misriyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dar_al-Ifta_al-Misriyyah) dubbed ISIS as "QSIS" for "al-Qaeda Separatists in Iraq and Syria", arguing that the group does not represent the vast majority of Muslims.[^cite_note-145]&#x20;

While the use of either one or the other [acronym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acronym) has been the subject of debate,[^cite_note-ISIS_or_ISIL?_The_debate-138] the distinction between the two and its relevance has been considered less important.[^cite_note-ISIS_or_ISIL?_The_debate-138] Of greater relevance is the name Daesh, which is an acronym of ISIL's [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) name *ad-Dawla al-Islamiyya fī l-ʿIrāq wa-sh-Shām*, or Daesh. This name is widely used among the Muslim world,[^cite_note-146] as well as ISIL's Arabic-speaking detractors,[^cite_note-BBC_2_December_2015-143] for example when referring to the group whilst speaking amongst themselves, although—and to a certain extent because⁠—it is considered derogatory, as it resembles the Arabic words *Daes* ("one who crushes, or tramples down, something underfoot") and *Dāhis* (loosely translated as "one who sows discord").[^cite_note-DAESH_shift-141][^cite_note-147] Within areas under its control, ISIL considers use of the name Daesh punishable by [flogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagellation).[^cite_note-148]&#x20;

In late June 2014, the group renamed itself *ad-Dawla al-Islāmiyya* (lit. 'Islamic State' or IS), declaring itself a worldwide [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate).[^cite_note-newname-128] The name "Islamic State" and the group's claim to be a caliphate have been widely rejected, with the UN, various governments, and mainstream Muslim groups refusing to use the new name.[^cite_note-Mandhai-149] The group's declaration of a new caliphate in June 2014 and its adoption of the name "Islamic State" have been criticised and ridiculed by Muslim scholars and rival Islamists both inside and outside the territory it controls.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

In a speech in September 2014, United States president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) said that ISIL was neither Islamic (on the basis that no religion condones the killing of innocents) nor a state (in that no government [recognises](524626.md) the group as a state),[^cite_note-potus-statement-151] while many object to using the name Islamic State owing to the far-reaching religious and political claims to authority which that name implies. The [United Nations Security Council](31956.md),[^cite_note-152] the United States,[^cite_note-potus-statement-151] Canada,[^cite_note-153] Turkey,[^cite_note-154] Australia,[^cite_note-155] the United Kingdom,[^cite_note-156] and other countries generally call the group ISIL, while much of the Arab world uses the [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) acronym Dāʻish or Daesh. France's Foreign Minister [Laurent Fabius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurent_Fabius) said: "This is a terrorist group and not a state. I do not recommend using the term Islamic State because it blurs the lines between Islam, Muslims, and Islamists. The Arabs call it 'Daesh' and I will be calling them the 'Daesh cutthroats'."[^cite_note-157] Retired general [John Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Allen), the U.S. envoy appointed to co-ordinate the coalition; [U.S. Army](32087.md) Lieutenant General [James Terry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Terry), head of operations against the group; and [Secretary of State](32293.md) [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) had all shifted towards use of the term Daesh by December 2014,[^cite_note-158] which nonetheless remained a pejorative in 2021.[^cite_note-159]&#x20;

## Purpose and strategy

### Ideology

Main article: [Ideology of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Islamic_State)

The [ideology of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Islamic_State) has been described as being a hybrid of [Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism),[^cite_note-Quilliam2014-32] [Salafi jihadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism),[^cite_note-Bunzel-31] [Islamic fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism),[^cite_note-Gerges-38] [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism),[^cite_note-Bunzel-31] and [Qutbism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutbism),[^cite_note-Poljarevic_chapter_2021-27] as well as other doctrines.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]&#x20;

According to Robert Manne, there is a "general consensus" that the ideology of the Islamic State is "primarily based upon the writings of the radical Egyptian theoretician [Sayyid Qutb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Qutb)".[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Yaqoubi2015xii-163] The Muslim Brotherhood began the trend of political Islamism in the 20th century, seeking gradual establishment of a new [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), a comprehensive Islamic society ruled by *[sharia](28840.md)* law. Qutb's doctrines of *[jahiliyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahiliyya)* (pre-Islamic ignorance), *hakimiyya* (divine sovereignty), and *[takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir)* of entire societies formed a radicalised vision of the Muslim Brotherhood's political Islam project. [Qutbism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutbism) became the precursor to all [jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist) thought, from [Abdullah Azzam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Yusuf_Azzam) to [Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zawahiri) and to Daesh.[^cite_note-164] Alongside Sayyid Qutb, the most invoked ideological figures of IS include [Ibn Taymiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Taymiyyah), Abdullah Azzam, and [Abu Bakr Naji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hasan_Khalil_al-Hakim).[^cite_note-165]&#x20;

Although IS claims to adhere to the [Salafi theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athari) of [Ibn Taymiyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Taymiyyah), it rejects traditional Salafi interpretations as well as the four Sunni schools of law, and anathematises the majority of Salafis as [heretics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heretics). IS ideologues rarely uphold adherence to Islamic scholarship and law manuals for reference, mostly preferring to derive rulings based on self-interpretation of the Qur'an and Muslim traditions.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Yaqoubi2015xiii,_11,_18-167][^cite_note-Poljarevic_Handbook_20212-168]&#x20;

[Abu Omar al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Omar_al-Baghdadi), the first Emir of the [Islamic State of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq), was radicalised as a Muslim Brotherhood member during his youth.[^cite_note-169] Motaz Al-Khateeb states that religious texts and Islamic jurisprudence "alone cannot explain the emergence" of Daesh since the Muslim Brotherhood and Daesh "draw on the same Islamic jurisprudence" but "are diametrically opposite" in strategy and behaviour.[^cite_note-170] Through the official statement of beliefs originally released by al-Baghdadi in 2007 and subsequently updated since June 2014, ISIL defined its [creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqidah) as "a middle way between the extremist [Kharijites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khawarij) and the lax [Murji'ites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murji%27ah)".[^cite_note-Bunzel-31]<sup>: 38 </sup> ISIL's ideology represents radical Jihadi-Salafi Islam, a strict, [puritanical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_puritanism) form of [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-171] Muslim organisations like Islamic Networks Group (ING) in America have argued against this interpretation of [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-172] ISIL promotes [religious violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_violence#Islam), and regards Muslims who do not agree with its interpretations as [infidels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafir) or [apostates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam).[^cite_note-ANS-173]&#x20;

According to Hayder al Khoei, IS's philosophy is represented by the symbolism in the [Black Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Standard) variant of the legendary battle flag of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) that it has adopted: [flag of IS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_ISIS) shows the [Seal of Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_Muhammad) within a white circle, with the phrase above it, "[There is no god but Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)".[^cite_note-time-2014-09-09-174] This symbolism is said to symbolise IS's belief that it represents the restoration of the [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) of [early Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_history_of_Islam), with all the political, religious, and [eschatological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology) ramifications that this would imply.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;

Abu Abdullah al-Muhajir, an Egyptian Jihadist theoretician and ideologue is considered as the key inspiration for early figures of IS.[^cite_note-Winter_2016-176][^cite_note-Townsend_2018-177][^cite_note-Ajjoub_2021-178] Al-Muhajir's legal manual on violence, *Fiqh ad-Dima* (*The Jurisprudence of Jihad* or *The Jurisprudence of Blood*),[^cite_note-AD-179][^cite_note-Winter_2016-176][^cite_note-Townsend_2018-177][^cite_note-Ajjoub_2021-178][^cite_note-180] was adopted by IS as its standard reference for justifying its extraordinary acts of violence.[^cite_note-AD-179][^cite_note-Winter_2016-176][^cite_note-Townsend_2018-177][^cite_note-Ajjoub_2021-178] The book has been described by counter-terrorism scholar Orwa Ajjoub as rationalising and justifying "suicide operations, the mutilation of corpses, beheading, and the killing of children and non-combatants".[^cite_note-Ajjoub_2021-178] His theological and legal justifications influenced IS,[^cite_note-Winter_2016-176][^cite_note-Townsend_2018-177][^cite_note-Ajjoub_2021-178] al-Qaeda,[^cite_note-Winter_2016-176] and [Boko Haram](23769768.md),[^cite_note-Townsend_2018-177] as well as several other jihadi terrorist groups.[^cite_note-Winter_2016-176] Media outlets have compared his reference manual to [Abu Bakr Naji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_Naji)'s *[Management of Savagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_of_Savagery)*,[^cite_note-newyorker-181][^cite_note-182] widely read among IS's commanders and fighters.[^cite_note-183]&#x20;

IS adheres to global jihadist principles and follows the hard-line ideology of [al-Qaeda](1921.md) and many other modern-day jihadist groups.&#x20;

> For their guiding principles, the leaders of the Islamic State ... are open and clear about their almost exclusive commitment to the Wahhabi movement of Sunni Islam. The group circulates images of Wahhabi religious textbooks from Saudi Arabia in the schools it controls. Videos from the group's territory have shown Wahhabi texts plastered on the sides of an official missionary van.

— David D. Kirkpatrick, *The New York Times*[^cite_note-ISIS_NYT-184]

According to *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, [Saudi](349303.md) practices followed by the group include the establishment of [religious police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_police) to root out "vice" and enforce attendance at [Salah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah) prayers, the widespread use of [capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Islam), and the destruction or re-purposing of any non-[Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) religious buildings.[^cite_note-185] [Bernard Haykel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Haykel) has described IS leader [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi)'s creed as "a kind of untamed Wahhabism".[^cite_note-ISIS_NYT-184] Senior Saudi religious leaders have issued statements condemning IS,[^cite_note-186] and attempting to distance the group from official Saudi religious beliefs.[^cite_note-187] What connection, if any, there is between Salafi-Jihadism of IS and Wahhabism and Salafism proper is disputed. IS borrowed two elements of [Qutbism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutbism) and 20th century Islamism into its version of Wahhabi worldview. While Wahhabism shuns violent rebellion against earthly rulers, IS embraces political call to revolutions. While historically Wahhabis were not champion activists of a [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), IS borrowed the idea of restoration of a global Caliphate.[^cite_note-188]&#x20;

Although the religious character of IS is mostly Wahhabi, it departs from the Wahhabi tradition in four critical aspects: dynastic alliance, call to establish a global caliphate, sheer violence, and apocalyptism.[^cite_note-189] IS did not follow the pattern of the first three Saudi states in allying the religious mission of the Najdi *[ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)* with the [Al Saud family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud), rather they consider them apostates. The call for a global caliphate is another departure from Wahhabism. The caliphate, understood in Islamic law as the ideal Islamic polity uniting all Muslim territories, does not figure much in traditional Najdi writings. Ironically, Wahhabism emerged as an anti-caliphate movement.[^cite_note-Bunzel_18_February_2016-190]&#x20;

Although [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence) was not absent in the First Saudi State, Islamic State's displays of beheading, immolation, and other forms of violence aimed at inspiring fear are not in imitation of early Saudi practices. They were introduced by Abu Musab Al-Zarqawi, former leader of Al-Qaeda in Iraq, who took inspiration from the Egyptian Jihadi scholar, Abu Abdallah Al Muhajir. It is the latter's legal manual on violence, popularly known as *Fiqh ad-Dima* (The Jurisprudence of Blood), that is the Islamic State's standard reference for justifying its acts of violence.[^cite_note-Bunzel_18_February_2016-190] The Islamic State's apocalyptic dimension also lacks a mainstream Wahhabi precedent.[^cite_note-Bunzel_18_February_2016-190]&#x20;

IS aims to return to the early days of Islam, rejecting all [innovations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bid%27ah) in the religion, which it believes corrupts its original spirit. It condemns later caliphates and the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) for deviating from what it calls pure Islam and seeks to revive the original Qutbist project of the restoration of a global caliphate that is governed by a strict Salafi-Jihadi doctrine. Following Salafi-Jihadi doctrines, IS condemns the followers of secular law as disbelievers, putting the current Saudi Arabian government in that category.&#x20;

IS believes that only a legitimate authority can undertake the leadership of [jihad](16203.md) and that the first priority over other areas of combat, such as fighting non-Muslim countries, is the purification of Islamic society. For example, IS regards the Palestinian Sunni group [Hamas](13913.md) as apostates who have no legitimate authority to lead jihad and see fighting Hamas as the first step towards confrontation by IS with Israel.[^cite_note-ISIS_NYT-184][^cite_note-191]&#x20;

Yemeni journalist [Abdulelah Haider Shaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulelah_Haider_Shaye) said:&#x20;

> The Islamic State was drafted by Sayyid Qutb, taught by Abdullah Azzam, globalized by Osama bin Laden, transferred to reality by Abu Musab al-Zarqawi, and implemented by al-Baghdadis: Abu Omar and Abu Bakr. \[...]&#x20;
>
> The Islamic State added a focus on sectarianism to a layer of radical views. In particular, it linked itself to the Salafi-jihadi movement that evolved out of the Afghan jihad.&#x20;

— Hassan Hassan, *The Sectarianism of the Islamic State: Ideological Roots and Political Context*.[^cite_note-Hassan_Hassan-192]

### Islamic eschatology

See also: [Islamic eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology)

One difference between IS and other Islamist and jihadist movements, including [al-Qaeda](1921.md), is the group's emphasis on [eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology) and [apocalypticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypticism)—that is, a belief in a final [Day of Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Judgment_in_Islam) by God. IS believes that it will defeat the army of "Rome" at the town of [Dabiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabiq,_Syria).[^cite_note-what-isis-really-wants-33]&#x20;

The noted scholar of militant Islamism [Will McCants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_McCants) writes:&#x20;

> References to the End Times fill Islamic State propaganda. It's a big selling point with foreign fighters, who want to travel to the lands where the final battles of the apocalypse will take place. The civil wars raging in those countries today \[Iraq and Syria] lend credibility to the prophecies. The Islamic State has stoked the apocalyptic fire. ... For Bin Laden's generation, the apocalypse wasn't a great recruiting pitch. Governments in the Middle East two decades ago were more stable, and sectarianism was more subdued. It was better to recruit by calling to arms against corruption and tyranny than against the Antichrist. Today, though, the apocalyptic recruiting pitch makes more sense than before.

— William McCants, *The ISIS Apocalypse: The History, Strategy, and Doomsday Vision of the Islamic State*[^cite_note-193]

### Goals

Since at latest 2004, a significant goal of the group has been the foundation of a Sunni [Islamic state](2646438.md).[^cite_note-194] Specifically, ISIL has sought to establish itself as a [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), an Islamic state led by a group of religious authorities under a supreme leader—the [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph)—who is believed to be the successor to [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad).[^cite_note-Johnson14-195] In June 2014, ISIL published a document in which it claimed to have traced the lineage of its leader al-Baghdadi back to Muhammad,[^cite_note-Johnson14-195] and upon proclaiming a new caliphate on 29 June, the group appointed al-Baghdadi as its caliph. As caliph, he demanded the allegiance of all devout Muslims worldwide according to Islamic jurisprudence (*[fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)*).[^cite_note-196]&#x20;

ISIL detailed its goals in its *[Dabiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabiq_\(magazine\))* magazine, saying it would continue to seize land and take over the entire Earth until its:&#x20;

> Blessed flag...covers all eastern and western extents of the Earth, filling the world with the truth and justice of Islam and putting an end to the falsehood and tyranny of jahiliyyah \[state of ignorance], even if America and its coalition despise such.

— 5th edition of *Dabiq*, the Islamic State's English-language magazine, in 2014[^cite_note-Joscelyn_2015-09-197]

According to German journalist [Jürgen Todenhöfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%BCrgen_Todenh%C3%B6fer), who spent ten days embedded with ISIL in [Mosul](200475.md), the view he kept hearing was that ISIL wants to "conquer the world", and that all who do not believe in the group's interpretation of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) will be killed. Todenhöfer was struck by the ISIL fighters' belief that "all religions who agree with democracy have to die",[^cite_note-Withnall-198] and by their "incredible enthusiasm"—including enthusiasm for killing "hundreds of millions" of people.[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

When the caliphate was proclaimed, ISIL stated: "The legality of all emirates, groups, states and organisations becomes null by the expansion of the khilafah's \[caliphate's] authority and the arrival of its troops to their areas."[^cite_note-Johnson14-195] This was a rejection of the political divisions in [Southwestern Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Asia) that were established by the UK and France during [World War I](4764461.md) in the [Sykes–Picot Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes%E2%80%93Picot_Agreement).[^cite_note-200]&#x20;

All non-Muslim areas would be targeted for conquest after the Muslim lands were dealt with, according to the Islamist manual *[Management of Savagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_of_Savagery)*.[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

### Strategy



The [Al-Askari Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Askari_Mosque), one of the holiest sites in Shia Islam, after [the first attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_al-Askari_mosque_bombing) by Islamic State of Iraq in 2006

Documents found after the death of Samir Abd Muhammad al-Khlifawi, a former colonel in the intelligence service of the [Iraqi Air Force](1817852.md) before the US invasion who had been described as "the strategic head" of ISIL, detailed planning for the ISIL takeover of northern Syria, which made possible "the group's later advances into Iraq". Al-Khlifawi called for the infiltration of areas to be conquered with spies who would find out "as much as possible about the target towns: Who lived there, who was in charge, which families were religious, which Islamic school of religious jurisprudence they belonged to, how many mosques there were, who the imam was, how many wives and children he had and how old they were". Following this surveillance and espionage would come murder and kidnapping—"the elimination of every person who might have been a potential leader or opponent". In [Raqqa](858965.md), after rebel forces drove out the [Assad regime](57134800.md) and ISIL infiltrated the town, "first dozens and then hundreds of people disappeared".[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

Security and intelligence expert Martin Reardon has described IS's purpose as being to psychologically "break" those under its control, "so as to ensure their absolute allegiance through fear and intimidation", while generating "outright hate and vengeance" among its enemies.[^cite_note-203] [Jason Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Burke), a journalist writing on [Salafi jihadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism), has written that IS's goal is to "terrorize, mobilize \[and] polarize".[^cite_note-Gude-2015-3-204] Its efforts to terrorise are intended to intimidate civilian populations and force governments of the target enemy "to make rash decisions that they otherwise would not choose". It aims to mobilise its supporters by motivating them with, for example, spectacular deadly attacks deep in Western territory (such as the [November 2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks)), to polarise by driving Muslim populations—particularly in the West—away from their governments, thus increasing the appeal of IS's self-proclaimed caliphate among them, and to: "Eliminate neutral parties through either absorption or elimination".[^cite_note-Gude-2015-3-204][^cite_note-205] Journalist [Rukmini Maria Callimachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukmini_Maria_Callimachi) also emphasises IS's interest in polarisation or in eliminating what it calls the "grey zone" between the black (non-Muslims) and white (IS). "The gray is moderate Muslims who are living in the West and are happy and feel engaged in the society here."[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

A work published online in 2004 entitled *[Management of Savagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_of_Savagery)*[^cite_note-207] (*Idarat at Tawahoush*), described by several media outlets as influential on IS[^cite_note-208] and intended to provide a strategy to create a new Islamic caliphate,[^cite_note-209] recommended a strategy of attack outside its territory in which fighters would "Diversify and widen the vexation strikes against the Crusader-Zionist enemy in every place in the Islamic world, and even outside of it if possible, so as to disperse the efforts of the alliance of the enemy and thus drain it to the greatest extent possible."[^cite_note-210]&#x20;

The group has been accused of attempting to "bolster morale" and distract attention from its loss of territory to enemies by staging terror attacks abroad (such as the [2016 Berlin truck attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Berlin_truck_attack), the [6 June 2017 attacks on Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Tehran_attacks), the [22 May 2017 bombing in Manchester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Arena_bombing), and the [3 June 2017 attacks in London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_London_Bridge_attack) that IS claimed credit for).[^cite_note-211]&#x20;

## Organisation

IS has been described as a militant group adhering to [Salafi jihadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism).[^cite_note-Bunzel-31][^cite_note-Quilliam2014-32][^cite_note-what-isis-really-wants-33][^cite_note-HuffPost_Wahhabi-34][^cite_note-Homegrown_2021-36][^cite_note-guardian-traincamp-114] [Raqqa](858965.md) in Syria was under IS control from 2013 and in 2014 it became the group's *de facto* capital city.[^cite_note-212] On 17 October 2017, following a lengthy battle that saw massive destruction to the city, the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) (SDF) announced the full capture of Raqqa from IS.[^cite_note-213]&#x20;

### Leadership and governance

Further information: [List of Islamic State members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Islamic_State_members)

From 2013 to 2019, IS was headed and run by [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi), the Islamic State's self-styled [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph). Before their deaths, he had two deputy leaders, [Abu Muslim al-Turkmani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Muslim_al-Turkmani) for Iraq and [Abu Ali al-Anbari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ali_al-Anbari) (also known as Abu Ala al-Afri)[^cite_note-214] for Syria, both ethnic [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Turkmen). Advising al-Baghdadi were a cabinet of senior leaders, while its operations in Iraq and Syria are controlled by local 'emirs,' who head semi-autonomous groups that the Islamic State refers to as its provinces.[^cite_note-215] Beneath the leaders are councils on finance, leadership, military matters, legal matters (including decisions on executions) [foreign fighters'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIL_foreign_fighters) assistance, security, intelligence and media. In addition, a [shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shura) council has the task of ensuring that all decisions made by the governors and councils comply with the group's interpretation of [sharia](28840.md).[^cite_note-216] While al-Baghdadi had told followers to "advise me when I err" in sermons, according to observers "any threat, opposition, or even contradiction is instantly eradicated".[^cite_note-NYRoB-7-9-2015-217]&#x20;

According to Iraqis, Syrians, and analysts who study the group, almost all of IS's leaders—including the members of its military and security committees and the majority of its [emirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir) and princes—are former Iraqi military and intelligence officers, specifically former members of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s [Ba'ath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Iraq_Region) government who lost their jobs and pensions in the [de-Ba'athification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Ba%27athification) process after that regime was overthrown.[^cite_note-Saddam-218][^cite_note-nytimes27Aug14-219] The former Chief Strategist in the Office of the Coordinator for Counterterrorism of the US State Department, [David Kilcullen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kilcullen), has said, "There undeniably would be no Isis if we had not invaded Iraq."[^cite_note-220] It has been reported that Iraqis and Syrians have been given greater precedence over other nationalities within IS because the group needs the loyalties of the local Sunni populations in both Syria and Iraq in order to be sustainable.[^cite_note-221] Other reports, however, have indicated that Syrians are at a disadvantage to foreign members, with some native Syrian fighters resenting "favouritism" allegedly shown towards foreigners over pay and accommodation.[^cite_note-222]&#x20;

In August 2016, media reports based on briefings by Western intelligence agencies suggested that IS had a multilevel [secret service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_service) known in Arabic as [Emni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emni), established in 2014, that has become a combination of an internal police force and an external operations directorate complete with regional branches. The unit was believed to be under the overall command of IS's most senior Syrian operative, spokesman and propaganda chief [Abu Mohammad al-Adnani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Mohammad_al-Adnani)[^cite_note-223] until his death by airstrike in late August 2016.[^cite_note-Chulov310816-224]&#x20;

On 27 October 2019, the United States conducted a special operation targeting al-Baghdadi's compound in [Barisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisha,_Harem_District), [Idlib](2528679.md), Northwest Syria. The attack [resulted in al-Baghdadi's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi); caught by surprise and unable to escape, al-Baghdadi detonated a [suicide vest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_vest), deliberately killing both himself and two children who had been living in the compound prior to the assault.[^cite_note-Browne-225][^cite_note-Al_Jazeera_2019-10-27-226] U.S. president Donald Trump stated in a televised announcement that Baghdadi had, in fact, died during the operation and that American forces used support from helicopters, jets and drones through airspace controlled by Russia and Turkey.[^cite_note-227] He said, "Russia treated us great... Iraq was excellent. We really had great cooperation" and Turkey knew they were going in.[^cite_note-Perraudin-228] He thanked Turkey, Russia, Syria, Iraq and the Syrian Kurdish forces for their support.[^cite_note-Perraudin-228] The Turkish Defence Ministry also confirmed on Sunday that Turkish and U.S. military authorities exchanged and coordinated information ahead of an attack in Syria's Idlib.[^cite_note-229] [Fahrettin Altun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahrettin_Altun), a senior aide to Turkish president Tayyib Erdogan, also stated, among other things, "Turkey was proud to help the United States, our NATO ally, bring a notorious terrorist to justice" and that Turkey "will continue to work closely with the United States and others to combat terrorism in all its forms and manifestations."[^cite_note-230] Kremlin spokesman [Dmitry Peskov](9214683.md) declined to say if the United States had told Russia about the raid in advance but said that its result if confirmed, represented a serious contribution by the United States to combat terrorism.[^cite_note-231] Russia had previously claimed Baghdadi was killed in May 2017 by their airstrike.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;

In September 2019, a statement attributed to IS's propaganda arm, the [Amaq news agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amaq_News_Agency), claimed that [Abdullah Qardash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Qardash) was named as al-Baghdadi's successor.[^cite_note-233] Analysts dismissed this statement as a fabrication, and relatives were reported as saying that Qardash died in 2017.[^cite_note-234] [Rita Katz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rita_Katz), a terrorism analyst and the co-founder of [SITE Intelligence](25696820.md), noted that the alleged statement used a different font when compared to other statements and it was never distributed on Amaq or IS channels.[^cite_note-235]&#x20;

On 29 October 2019, Trump stated on social media that al-Baghdadi's "number one replacement" had been killed by American forces, without giving a name.[^cite_note-236] A U.S. official later confirmed that Trump was referring to IS spokesman and senior leader [Abul-Hasan al-Muhajir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul-Hasan_al-Muhajir),[^cite_note-237] who was killed in a U.S. airstrike in Syria two days earlier.[^cite_note-238] On 31 October, IS named [Abu Ibrahim al-Hashemi al-Qurayshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ibrahim_al_Hashimi_al-Qurayshi) as Baghdadi's successor.[^cite_note-239] On 3 February 2022, it was reported by a US official that al-Hashimi killed himself and members of his family by triggering an explosive device, during a [counter-terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism) raid by the US [Joint Special Operations Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Special_Operations_Command).[^cite_note-240] On 30 November 2022, IS announced that their next leader, [Abu al-Hasan al-Hashimi al-Qurashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_al-Hasan_al-Hashimi_al-Qurashi), had been killed in battle and named a successor, providing no additional information other than his [pseudonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudonym), [Abu al-Hussein al-Husseini al-Qurashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_al-Hussein_al-Husseini_al-Qurashi), who was believed to be the Islamic State's first Syrian chief.[^cite_note-241] A spokesman for [U.S. Central Command](338482.md) confirmed that IS's leader had been killed in mid-October by anti-government rebels in southern Syria.[^cite_note-242] On 16 February 2023, senior IS leader Hamza al-Homsi blew himself up in a U.S.-led raid in Syria.[^cite_note-243] On 23 April 2023, IS leader Abu al-Hussein al-Husseini al-Qurashi was killed in an attack on his residence at [Jindires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jindires), in the northwestern [Idlib Governorate](7445043.md). Turkey claimed responsibility for his death, while the Islamic State claimed that al-Husseini al-Qurashi was killed in a shootout with [Tahrir al-Sham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_al-Sham) (HTS) forces. Subsequently, the Islamic State named [Abu Hafs al-Hashimi al-Qurashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hafs_al-Hashimi_al-Qurashi) as their new leader.[^cite_note-Turkey_offers_details-244][^cite_note-ISIL_confirms_death-13] In late August 2025, US Special Forces carried out a helicopter raid at [Atmeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmeh), near the Turkish border region. They killed two senior IS leaders in the raid, including a figure who was believed to be next-in-line for the Islamic State leadership.[^cite_note-US_raid-14] Analysts believe that the Islamic State is headquartered in the northwestern [Idlib Governorate](7445043.md) region, near the Turkish border, ever since the group [lost their last territorial stronghold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Baghuz_Fawqani) at [Al-Baghuz Fawqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Baghuz_Fawqani) in Spring 2019.[^cite_note-245]&#x20;

### &#x20;Civilians in Islamic State-controlled areas

Main article: [Human rights in Islamic State-controlled territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Islamic_State-controlled_territory)

Further information: [Collaboration with the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_with_the_Islamic_State)

In 2014, *The Wall Street Journal* estimated that eight million people lived in the Islamic State.[^cite_note-246] The [United Nations Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Commission_on_Human_Rights) has stated that IS "seeks to subjugate civilians under its control and dominate every aspect of their lives through terror, indoctrination, and the provision of services to those who obey".[^cite_note-UNRuleOfTerror-247] Civilians, as well as the Islamic State itself, have released footage of some of the human rights abuses.[^cite_note-248]&#x20;

[Social control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_control) of civilians was imposed with IS's strict interpretation of sharia law,[^cite_note-McCoyTop-249] enforced by [morality police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_police) forces known as *Al-Hisbah* and the all-women [Al-Khanssaa Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Khanssaa_Brigade), a general police force, courts, and other entities managing recruitment, tribal relations, and education.[^cite_note-UNRuleOfTerror-247] *Al-Hisbah* was led by Abu Muhammad al-Jazrawi.[^cite_note-250]&#x20;

In 2015, IS published a penal code including floggings, amputations, crucifixions, etc.[^cite_note-251]&#x20;

### Military

Main articles: [Military of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_the_Islamic_State) and [List of military equipment of Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_equipment_of_Islamic_State)

#### Number of combatants

| Country | Fighters |
| --- | --- |
| Russia | 5,000 |
| Tunisia | 4,000 |
| Jordan | 3,950 |
| Saudi Arabia | 3,244 |
| Turkey | 3,000 |
| Uzbekistan | 2,500 |
| France | 1,910 |
| Morocco | 1,699 |
| Tajikistan | 1,502 |
| China | 1,000 |
| Germany | 960 |
| Lebanon | 900 |
| Azerbaijan | 900 |
| Kyrgyzstan | 863 |
| United Kingdom | 860 |
| Indonesia | 800 |
| Kazakhstan | 600 |
| Libya | 600 |
| Egypt | 500 |
| Turkmenistan | 500 |
| Belgium | 500 |

Estimates of the size of IS's military have varied widely, from tens of thousands[^cite_note-253] up to 200,000. In early 2015, journalist Mary Anne Weaver estimated that half of IS fighters were foreigners.[^cite_note-254] A UN report estimated a total of 15,000 fighters from over 80 countries were in IS's ranks in November 2014.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] US intelligence estimated an increase to around 20,000 foreign fighters in February 2015, including 3,400 from the [Western world](21208200.md).[^cite_note-257] In September 2015, the CIA estimated that 30,000 foreign fighters had joined IS.[^cite_note-258]&#x20;

According to Abu Hajjar, a former senior leader of IS, foreign fighters receive food, petrol and housing, but unlike native Iraqi or Syrian fighters, they do not receive payment in wages.[^cite_note-259] Since 2012, more than 3,000 people from the central Asian countries have gone to Syria, Iraq or Afghanistan to join the Islamic State or [Jabhat al Nusra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabhat_Al-Nusra).[^cite_note-260]&#x20;

#### &#x20;Conventional weapons

IS relies mostly on captured weapons with major sources including [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s Iraqi stockpiles from the [2003–11 Iraq insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%9311\))[^cite_note-261] and weapons from government and opposition forces fighting in the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) and during the [post-US withdrawal Iraqi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2011%E2%80%93present\)). The captured weapons, including armour, guns, surface-to-air missiles, and even some aircraft, enabled rapid territorial growth and facilitated the capture of additional equipment.[^cite_note-262] For example, IS captured US-made [TOW anti-tank missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BGM-71_TOW) supplied by the United States and Saudi Arabia to the [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army) in Syria.[^cite_note-263] Ninety per cent of the group's weapons ultimately originated in China, Russia or [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) according to [Conflict Armament Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_Armament_Research).[^cite_note-264]&#x20;

#### Non-conventional weapons

The group uses truck and [car bombs](155282.md), [suicide bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_bombers) and [IEDs](265112.md), and has used [chemical weapons](27179600.md) in Iraq and Syria.[^cite_note-265] IS captured nuclear materials from [Mosul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosul_University) in July 2014, but is unlikely to be able to convert them into weapons.[^cite_note-266] In September 2015 a US official stated that IS was manufacturing and using [mustard agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustard_agent) in Syria and Iraq, and had an active chemical weapons research team.[^cite_note-267] IS has also used water as a weapon of war. The group closed the gates of the smaller Nuaimiyah dam in Fallujah in April 2014, flooding the surrounding regions, while cutting the water supply to the [Shia](26961.md)-dominated south. Around 12,000 families lost their homes and 200 square kilometres (77 mi<sup>2</sup>) of villages and fields were either flooded or dried up. The economy of the region also suffered with destruction of cropland and electricity shortages.[^cite_note-268] During the [Battle of Mosul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Mosul_\(2016%E2%80%9317\)), commercially available [quadcopters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadcopter) and [drones](58900.md) were being used by IS as surveillance and weapons delivery platforms using improvised cradles to drop grenades and other explosives.[^cite_note-269] One IS drone base was struck and destroyed by two Royal Air Force [Tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panavia_Tornado) using two [Paveway IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paveway_IV) guided bombs.[^cite_note-270]&#x20;

### Women

See also: [Brides of the Islamic State](60043985.md)

IS publishes material directed at women, with media groups encouraging them to play supportive roles within IS, such as providing first aid, cooking, nursing and sewing skills, in order to become ["good wives of jihad"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brides_of_ISIL).[^cite_note-271] In 2015, it was estimated that western women made up over 550, or 10%, of IS's western foreign fighters.[^cite_note-272]&#x20;

Until 2016, women were generally confined to a "women's house" upon arrival, which they were forbidden to leave. These houses were often small, dirty and infested with vermin and food supply was scarce. There they remained until they either had found a husband, or the husband they arrived with had completed his training. After being allowed to leave the confinement, women still generally spent most of their days indoors, where their lives are devoted to caring for their husbands and the vast majority of women in the conflict area have children. Mothers play an important role passing on IS ideology to their children. Widows are encouraged to remarry.[^cite_note-273]&#x20;

In a document entitled *Women in the Islamic State: Manifesto and Case Study* released by the media wing of IS's all-female [Al-Khansaa Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Khansaa_Brigade), emphasis is given to the paramount importance of marriage and motherhood (as early as nine years old). Women should live a life of "sedentariness", fulfilling her "divine duty of motherhood" at home, with a few exceptions like teachers and doctors.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-quilliam-women-275] Equality for women is opposed, as is education on non-religious subjects, the "worthless worldly sciences".[^cite_note-quilliam-women-275]&#x20;

### Communications

Main article: [Mass media use by the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_use_by_the_Islamic_State)

### Finances

Main article: [Finances of the Islamic State](48632799.md)

See also: [Kuwait and state-sponsored terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwait_and_state-sponsored_terrorism), [Qatar and state-sponsored terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_and_state-sponsored_terrorism), and [Oil production and smuggling in the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_production_and_smuggling_in_the_Islamic_State)

According to a 2015 study by the [Financial Action Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Action_Task_Force), IS's five primary sources of revenue are as follows (listed in order of significance):&#x20;

- proceeds from the occupation of territory (including control of banks, petroleum reservoirs, taxation, extortion, and robbery of economic assets)

- kidnapping for ransom

  [^cite_note-276]

- donations from [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia_and_state-sponsored_terrorism), [Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwait_and_state-sponsored_terrorism), [Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_and_state-sponsored_terrorism) and other [Gulf states](3088555.md), often disguised as meant for "humanitarian charity"

- material support provided by foreign fighters

- fundraising through modern communication networks

  [^cite_note-FATF-277]

Since 2012, IS has produced annual reports giving numerical information on its operations, somewhat in the style of corporate reports, seemingly in a bid to encourage potential donors.[^cite_note-ft-20140617-278][^cite_note-279]&#x20;

In 2014, the [RAND Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND_Corporation) analysed IS's funding sources from documents captured between 2005 and 2010.[^cite_note-ISIfunding-280] It found that outside donations amounted to only 5% of the group's operating budgets,[^cite_note-ISIfunding-280] and that cells inside Iraq were required to send up to 20% of the income generated from kidnapping, extortion rackets and other activities to the next level of the group's leadership, which would then redistribute the funds to provincial or local cells that were in difficulties or needed money to conduct attacks.[^cite_note-ISIfunding-280] In 2016, RAND estimated that IS finances from its largest source of income—oil revenues and the taxes it extracts from people under its control—had fallen from about US\$1.9 billion in 2014 to US\$870 million in 2016.[^cite_note-281]&#x20;

In mid-2014, the [Iraqi National Intelligence Service](1012921.md) obtained information that IS had assets worth US\$2 billion,[^cite_note-282] making it the richest jihadist group in the world.[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] About three-quarters of this sum was said to looted from Mosul's central bank and commercial banks in the city.[^cite_note-285] However, doubt was later cast on whether IS was able to retrieve anywhere near that sum from the central bank,[^cite_note-286] and even on whether the looting had actually occurred.[^cite_note-287]&#x20;

In 2022, the company [Lafarge](41902084.md) was found guilty in paying IS for the operation of its facilities. "In 2013–2014 the company transferred \$6,000,000 to ISIL so they could continue company operations. This allowed the company to earn \$70 million in sales revenue from a plant it operated in northern Syria, prosecutors said." Lafarge, which merged with [Holcim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holcim) in 2015, agreed to pay \$778 million in forfeiture and fines as part of a plea agreement not to be convicted and sentenced to prison for providing material support to a terrorist organisation. No Lafarge executives were charged in the United States, while French authorities arrested some of the executives involved but didn't provide names. The U.S. court lists six unnamed Lafarge executives. Lafarge evacuated the cement plant in September 2014, Afterwards IS took possession of the remaining cement and sold it for an estimated \$3.21 million. [SIX Swiss Exchange trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=SIX_Swiss_Exchange_trading\&action=edit\&redlink=1) suspended trading for Holcim shares before the news became public. After trading resumed shares rose by 3.2%.[^cite_note-288]&#x20;

#### Monetary system

IS attempted to create a [modern gold dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_dinar) by minting gold, silver, and copper coins, based on the [coinage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_dinar) used by the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) in the seventh century.[^cite_note-289] Despite a propaganda push for the currency, adoption appeared to have been minimal and its internal economy was effectively [dollarised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dollarised), even with regards to its own fines.[^cite_note-290]&#x20;

#### Education

The education in IS held territory was organised by the Diwan of Education.[^cite_note-Qaddour_2017-10-13-291][^cite_note-Education_in_Mosul-292] IS introduced its own curriculum, which did not include lessons in history, music, geography or art, but included lectures in Islamic Law, [Sharia](28840.md), and [Jihad](16203.md).[^cite_note-Education_in_Mosul-292] The Diwan of Education was often in competition with the Diwan of Outreach and Mosques, which organised educational centres focused on the sharia.[^cite_note-Qaddour_2017-10-13-291]&#x20;

[Iman al-Bugha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iman_al-Bugha) may have been chief of education under the caliphate. On social media she described what the education system was like: five years of primary education and four years of secondary, with “artificial and taboo subjects” prohibited. Students were supposed to be able to attend university at age fifteen and graduate by eighteen.[^cite_note-293]&#x20;

## &#x20;Origins

Main article: [History of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Islamic_State)



The UN headquarters building in Baghdad after the [Canal Hotel bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Hotel_bombing), on 22 August 2003

The group was founded in 1999 by Jordanian [Salafi jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafist_jihadism) [Abu Musab al-Zarqawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Zarqawi) under the name *[Jamāʻat al-Tawḥīd wa-al-Jihād](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jama%27at_al-Tawhid_wal-Jihad)* (lit. 'The Organisation of Monotheism and Jihad').[^cite_note-winepJune14-294] In a letter published by the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) in February 2004, Zarqawi wrote that jihadis should use bombings to start an open [sectarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian) war so that Sunnis from the Islamic world would mobilise against assassinations carried out by [Shia](26961.md), specifically the [Badr Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badr_Organization), against [Ba'athists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%27athists) and [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnis).[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296][^cite_note-ANF-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301]&#x20;

It is possible that Zarqawi used some "small seed money"[^cite_note-Zelin-302] of \$200,000[^cite_note-303] obtained from [Al Qaeda](1921.md) to set up a training camp for the JTJ.[^cite_note-Zelin-302] The financial support from Al Qaeda came after Zarqawi was reluctant to be recruited by [bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden).[^cite_note-304] Following the [11 September Attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_11_Attacks) and the decision to [invade Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Iraq), the [U.S. Secretary of State's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) [presentation to the United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell%27s_presentation_to_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) aimed to establish Zarqawi as the definitive link between Al Qaeda and Iraq. These claims were later disproven.[^cite_note-305]&#x20;

Powell's presentation was nevertheless instrumental in elevating Zarqawi's status and laying the groundwork for part of the foreign fighter faction of the [Iraqi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(disambiguation\)).[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-Caliphate-307]&#x20;

The JTJ officially pledged allegiance to al-Qaeda in a letter in October 2004, which drew more recruits, as well as financial and logistical support.[^cite_note-Caliphate-307]&#x20;

## Territorial control and claims

Main articles: [Territory of the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_the_Islamic_State) and [Territory of the Islamic State – West Africa Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_the_Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_West_Africa_Province)



[IS territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_the_Islamic_State), in grey, at the time of its greatest territorial extent (May 2015).

[^cite_note-nytimes_2015-05-15-108]

Map legend

- &#x20; Islamic State

- &#x20; [Iraqi government](11752991.md)

- &#x20; Syrian government

- &#x20; Lebanese government

- &#x20; [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) forces

- &#x20; [Syrian Kurdistan forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria)

- &#x20; [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition) forces

- &#x20; [Turkish Armed Forces](30205.md)

- &#x20; [Al-Nusra Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nusra_Front)

- &#x20; [Hezbollah](13919.md)

- **Note:** Iraq and Syria contain large desert areas with sparse populations. These areas are mapped as under the control of forces holding roads and towns within them.



Military situation in Libya in early 2016:\
 [Ansar al-Sharia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansar_al-Sharia_\(Libya\))  IS

As a self-proclaimed worldwide [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), IS claims religious, political and military authority over all Muslims worldwide, and that "the legality of all emirates, groups, states, and organisations, becomes null by the expansion of the khilāfah's \[caliphate's] authority and arrival of its troops to their areas".[^cite_note-Johnson14-195]&#x20;

In Iraq and Syria, IS used many of those countries' existing [governorate boundaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Iraq) to subdivide territory it conquered and claimed; it called these divisions [wilayah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilayah) or provinces.[^cite_note-308] By June 2015, IS had also established official "provinces" in Libya, Egypt (Sinai Peninsula), Saudi Arabia, Yemen, Algeria, Afghanistan,[^cite_note-309] Pakistan, Nigeria and the North Caucasus.[^cite_note-310] IS received pledges of allegiance and published media releases via groups in Somalia,[^cite_note-311] Bangladesh,[^cite_note-312] Indonesia, Myanmar,[^cite_note-313] Thailand[^cite_note-314] and the Philippines,[^cite_note-315] but it has not announced any further official branches, instead identifying new affiliates as simply "soldiers of the caliphate".[^cite_note-316]&#x20;



IS's capital [Raqqa](858965.md) suffered extensive damage during the [battle of Raqqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Raqqa_\(2017\)) in June–October 2017

At its peak, IS was sometimes described as a [quasi-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quasi-state).[^cite_note-Theocratic_State-317] By March 2019, IS had lost most of its territory in its former core areas in Syria and Iraq, and was reduced to a desert pocket as well as insurgent cells.[^cite_note-318] Through late 2020 and early 2021, IS African affiliates had once again seized territory and settlements in conflicts such as the [Boko Haram insurgency](27868253.md), in Nigeria and the [Insurgency in Cabo Delgado](56119804.md), in Mozambique. Notable takeovers by IS include [Mocímboa da Praia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moc%C3%ADmboa_da_Praia_offensive) and the [Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sambisa_Forest_\(2021\)).[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] On 17 November 2021, IS supporters urged establishment of "New Provinces" in Indonesia.[^cite_note-321] In October 2022, IS's Sahel province captured the rural committee and town of [Ansongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansongo) in Mali.[^cite_note-322] In 2023–2024, IS still held a large territory in Africa.[^cite_note-C-T-K-123][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20242,_6-124]&#x20;

## IS affiliates

### In Africa

- [Islamic State – Central Africa Province (IS-CAP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Central_Africa_Province) first appeared in 2018 and operates in the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md) and [Uganda](31816.md) since 2020. It emerged from the Ugandan Islamist group [Allied Democratic Forces](1258240.md) (ADF). As of 2024, it is estimated to number 1,500-2,000 fighters.

  [^cite_note-323]

  [^cite_note-324]

- [Islamic State – Mozambique Province (ISMP)](79489957.md), formerly part of Islamic State – Central Africa Province (IS-CAP), was formed in 2022. It is primarily active in [Mozambique](19301.md)'s [Cabo Delgado](448193.md) province. It emerged from the Mozambican Islamist group [Al-Shabaab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shabaab_\(Mozambique\)). Its current size is unknown.

- [Islamic State – Sahel Province (ISSP)](55577612.md), formerly known as Islamic State in the Greater Sahara (IS-GS), operates mainly in [Mali](19127.md), [Niger](21373.md) and [Burkina Faso](3470.md). IS-GS was formed in 2015. From March 2019 to 2022, IS-GS was formally part of the Islamic State – West Africa Province (ISWAP); when it was also called "ISWAP-Greater Sahara". As of 2025, ISSP is estimated to have 2,000-3,000 fighters.

  [^cite_note-325]

- [Islamic State – Somalia Province (ISSP)](53150866.md) operates primarily in [Puntland](233569.md) in northern [Somalia](27358.md). The group first appeared in 2015 when elements within the Somali Islamist group [Al-Shabaab](8726463.md) defected and pledged allegiance to IS.

  [^cite_note-326]

  &#x20;Its size is estimated to be 100-200 fighters as of 2025.

  [^cite_note-327]

- [Islamic State – West Africa Province (ISWAP)](46529902.md) is primarily active in the [Chad Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad_Basin) of Niger, [Nigeria](21383.md), [Cameroon](5447.md) and [Chad](5488.md). It emerged in 2015 after a split from [Boko Haram](23769768.md). As of 2025, ISWAP is estimated to have 8,000-12,000 fighters.

  [^cite_note-328]

### In Asia

- [Islamic State – Azerbaijan Province (IS-AP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Azerbaijan_Province) emerged in 2019 and operates in [Azerbaijan](746.md). Its adeherents were previously recruited by both the Islamic State – Caucasus Province (IS-CP) and Islamic State – Khorasan Province (ISIS-K).

  [^cite_note-329]

  &#x20;Its current size is unknown.

- [Islamic State – Bengal Province (IS-BP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Bengal_Province) is active in [Bangladesh](3454.md). The group is an offshoot of the [Al-Qaeda](1921.md) affiliated [Jamaat-ul-Mujahideen Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat-ul-Mujahideen_Bangladesh) (JMB).

  [^cite_note-330]

  &#x20;It emerged in 2015 and was claimed by IS in 2016.

  [^cite_note-331]

  &#x20;Its current size is unknown.

- [Islamic State – Hind Province (ISHP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Hind_Province) is the [Indian](14533.md) branch of IS and operates in [Indian Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md). Its formation was announced in 2019. Its current size is unknown.

- [Islamic State – Khorasan Province (ISIS-K)](48029263.md) emerged in 2015 and operates in [Central](6742.md) and [South Asia](21566765.md), primarily [Afghanistan](737.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Tajikistan](30108.md) and [Uzbekistan](31853.md). As of 2025, ISIS-K is estimated to have 2,000 fighters.

  [^cite_note-332]

  [^cite_note-333]

- [Islamic State – Pakistan Province (ISPP)](77167398.md) was formed in 2019 as a split from Islamic State – Khorasan Province (ISIS-K). It is active in the Pakistani provinces of [Punjab](24751.md), [Balochistan](4687.md) and [Azad Kashmir](2745.md), as well as [Iran](14653.md)'s [Sistan and Baluchestan](448105.md) province. Its current size is unknown.

- [Islamic State – Philippines Province (ISPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Philippines_Province) operates in the [Philippines](23440.md), primarily in [Mindanao](20546.md). It emerged in 2016 from local jihadist groups [Maute Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maute_group), [Bangsamoro Islamic Freedom Fighters](39963003.md) (BIFF), and [Ansar Khalifa Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansar_Khalifa_Philippines) (AKP).

  [^cite_note-334]

- [Islamic State – Turkey Province (IS-TP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Turkey_Province) was formed in 2019 and is active in [Turkey](11125639.md). It is less active than other IS affiliates.

  [^cite_note-335]

  &#x20;Its current size is unknown.

### In Europe

- [Islamic State – Caucasus Province (IS-CP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Caucasus_Province) is active in the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus) region of [Russia](25391.md). IS announced the group's formation in 2015.

  [^cite_note-336]

  [^cite_note-337]

  &#x20;Its current size is unknown.

## &#x20;International reaction

### International criticism

The group has attracted widespread criticism internationally for its extremism, from governments and international bodies such as the [United Nations](31769.md) and [Amnesty International](18947898.md). On 24 September 2014, [United Nations Secretary-General](162415.md) [Ban Ki-moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon) stated: "As Muslim leaders around the world have said, groups like ISIL—or Da'ish—have nothing to do with [Islam](6037917.md), and they certainly do not represent a state. They should more fittingly be called the 'Un-Islamic Non-State'."[^cite_note-338] ISIL has been classified a [terrorist organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_designated_terrorist_organizations) by the United Nations, the European Union and its member states, the United States, Russia, India, Turkey, Saudi Arabia and many other countries. Over 60 countries are directly or indirectly waging war against ISIL (see [§ Countries and groups at war with IS](#Countries_and_groups_at_war_with_IS)). The group was described as a [cult](1008588.md) in a [Huffington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huffington_Post) column by notable cult authority [Steven Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Hassan).[^cite_note-\:0-113]&#x20;

Twitter has removed many accounts used to spread IS propaganda, and [Google](1092923.md) developed a "[Redirect Method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redirect_Method)" that identifies individuals searching for IS-related material and redirects them to content that challenges IS narratives.[^cite_note-339]&#x20;

### Islamic criticism

See also: [Letter to Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letter_to_Baghdadi)

The group's declaration of a caliphate has been criticised and its legitimacy has been disputed by Middle Eastern governments, by [Sunni Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslim) theologians and historians as well as other jihadist groups.[^cite_note-340]&#x20;

#### Religious leaders and organisations

See also: [Kharijites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites)

Around the world, [Islamic religious leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_leaders) have overwhelmingly condemned ISIL's ideology and actions, arguing that the group has strayed from the path of true Islam and that its actions do not reflect the religion's real teachings or virtues.[^cite_note-ns10Mar2015-341]&#x20;

Extremism within Islam goes back to the seventh century, to the [Kharijites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites). From their essentially political position, the Kharijites developed extreme doctrines that set them apart from both mainstream Sunni and Shia Muslims. They were particularly noted for adopting a radical approach to [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir), whereby they declared other Muslims to be unbelievers and therefore deemed worthy of death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Yaqoubi2015xvii–xviii-342][^cite_note-SheemaKhan-343] Other scholars have also described the group not as Sunnis, but as Khawarij.[^cite_note-SheemaKhan-343][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Yaqoubi2015xvii–xviii,_see_also_p.8-344] Sunni critics, including [Salafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement) and jihadist [muftis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muftis) such as [Adnan al-Aroor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adnan_al-Aroor) and [Abu Basir al-Tartusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Basir_al-Tartusi), say that ISIL and related terrorist groups are not Sunnis, but are instead modern-day Kharijites (Muslims who have stepped outside the mainstream of Islam) serving an imperial anti-Islamic agenda.[^cite_note-econ140906-345]&#x20;

ISIS has been [excommunicated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir) from Islam by a number of scholars. Sheikh [Muhammad al-Yaqoubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Yaqoubi) enumerated in his book, *[Refuting ISIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refuting_ISIS)*, that their form of Kharijism has removed them from Islam and fighting them is a religious duty, stating: "ISIS' leaders are people of unbelief and misguidance, and Muslims should not be lured by their jihad or deceived by their propaganda, as their actions speak louder than their words."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Yaqoubi201575–80-346] [Abd al-Aziz ibn Baz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Aziz_ibn_Baz), the former Grand Mufti of [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), also stated that Kharijites are not Muslims, saying: "the majority are of the opinion that they are disobedient and misguided innovators, though they do not deem them unbelievers. However, the correct opinion is that they are unbelievers."[^cite_note-347]&#x20;

&#x20;In late August 2014, the [Grand Mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti) of Saudi Arabia, [Abdul-Aziz ibn Abdullah Al ash-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Aziz_ibn_Abdullah_Al_ash-Sheikh), condemned ISIL and [al-Qaeda](1921.md) saying, "Extremist and militant ideas and terrorism which spread decay on Earth, destroying human civilisation, are not in any way part of Islam, but are enemy number one of Islam, and Muslims are their first victims".[^cite_note-348] In late September 2014, 126 Sunni [imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imams) and Islamic scholars—primarily [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism)[^cite_note-349]—from around the Muslim world signed an [open letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_letter) to the Islamic State's leader al-Baghdadi, explicitly rejecting and refuting his group's interpretations of Islamic scriptures, the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith), which it used in order to justify its actions.[^cite_note-350] "\[You] have misinterpreted Islam into a religion of harshness, brutality, torture and murder ... this is a great wrong and an offence to Islam, to Muslims and to the entire world", the letter states.[^cite_note-OpenLetToAlBagh-351] It rebukes the Islamic State for its killing of prisoners, describing the killings as "heinous [war crimes](48626.md)" and its persecution of the [Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidis) of Iraq as "abominable". Referring to the "self-described 'Islamic State'", the letter censures the group for carrying out killings and acts of brutality under the guise of [jihad](16203.md)—holy struggle—saying that its "sacrifice" without legitimate cause, goals and intention "is not jihad at all, but rather, warmongering and criminality".[^cite_note-OpenLetToAlBagh-351][^cite_note-352] It also accuses the group of instigating [fitna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitna_\(word\))—sedition—by instituting slavery under its rule in contravention of the [anti-slavery consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_slavery#Modern_interpretations) of the [Islamic scholarly community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama).[^cite_note-OpenLetToAlBagh-351] The group's persecution of Shia Muslims has also been condemned.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-DW\.COM_AI-355]&#x20;



Pro-[YPG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YPG) demonstration against ISIL in [Vienna](55866.md), Austria, 10 October 2014

The current [Grand Imam of al-Azhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Imam_of_al-Azhar) and former president of [al-Azhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_University), [Ahmed el-Tayeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_el-Tayeb), has strongly condemned the Islamic State, stating that it is acting "under the guise of this holy religion and have given themselves the name 'Islamic State' in an attempt to export their false Islam".[^cite_note-356] Citing the Quran, he stated: "The punishment for those who wage war against God and his Prophet and who strive to sow corruption on earth is death, crucifixion, the severing of hands and feet on opposite sides or banishment from the land. This is the disgrace for them in this world and in the hereafter, they will receive grievous torment." Although el-Tayeb has been criticised for not expressly stating that the Islamic State is [heretical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heretical),[^cite_note-AhramRenewal-357] the [Ash'ari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash%27ari) school of [Islamic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_theology), to which el-Tayeb belongs, does not allow calling a person who follows the [shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada) an [apostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostate).[^cite_note-AhramRenewal-357] El-Tayeb has strongly come out against the practice of [takfirism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfirism) (declaring a Muslim an apostate), which is used by the Islamic State to "judge and accuse anyone who doesn't tow their line with apostasy and outside the realm of the faith" declaring "[Jihad](16203.md) on peaceful Muslims" using "flawed interpretations of some Qur'anic texts, the prophet's Sunna, and the Imams' views believing incorrectly, that they are leaders of Muslim armies fighting infidel peoples, in unbelieving lands".[^cite_note-358]&#x20;

In late December 2015, nearly 70,000 Indian Muslim clerics associated with the Indian [Barelvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barelvi) movement issued a [fatwa](11259.md) condemning ISIL and similar organisations, saying they are "not Islamic organisations". Approximately 1.5 million Sunni Muslim followers of this movement have formally decried violent extremists.[^cite_note-359]&#x20;

[Mehdi Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Hasan), a political journalist in the UK, said in the *[New Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Statesman)*&#x20;

> Whether Sunni or Shia, Salafi or Sufi, conservative or liberal, Muslims—and Muslim leaders—have almost unanimously condemned and denounced ISIL not merely as un-Islamic but actively anti-Islamic.[^cite_note-ns10Mar2015-341]

Hassan Hassan, an analyst at the Delma Institute, wrote in *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* that because the Islamic State "bases its teachings on religious texts that mainstream Muslim clerics do not want to deal with head on, new recruits leave the camp feeling that they have stumbled on the true message of Islam".[^cite_note-guardian-traincamp-114]&#x20;

Theologian and Qatar-based TV broadcaster [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi) stated: "\[The] declaration issued by the Islamic State is void under [sharia](28840.md) and has dangerous consequences for the Sunnis in Iraq and for the revolt in Syria", adding that the title of caliph can "only be given by the entire Muslim nation", not by a single group.[^cite_note-360] He also stated on his official website "[United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (UAE) and the leaders of Daesh (ISIS/ISIL) terrorist group are from one species and they are two sides of the same coin".[^cite_note-361] In a similar vein, the Syrian [Islamic scholar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama) [Muhammad al-Yaqoubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Yaqoubi) says, "\[t]he followers of ISIS do not want to adhere to Islamic law but rather they want to twist Islamic law to conform to their fantasies.
